Document 002: 10年期美债突破5%！“两种叙事”萦绕市场：“2023年式短暂见顶”还是“2000年代式引爆金融危机”

Source PDF: all report pdf/10年期美债突破5%！“两种叙事”萦绕市场：“2023年式短暂见顶”还是“2000年代式引爆金融危机”.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-09-15
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

美国 10 年期国债收益率隔夜盘中突破 5% ，创 2007 年以来新⾼。当前市场存在两种截然对
⽴的叙事，⼀种认为 5% 将如 2023 年 10 ⽉那般成为阶段性顶部，随后重新回落；另⼀种则
担忧收益率将决定性地突破这⼀⻔槛，复现 2000 年代乃⾄ 1990 年代收益率⻓期⾼企的格
局。美债⾛势将对消费者、企业乃⾄美国政府的借贷成本产⽣深远影响，并可能左右中期
选举⾛向。
摘要：
美国 10 年期国债收益率作为全球数万亿美元资产的基准，受伊朗战争冲击飙升⾄ 5% ，这被普遍
认为是⼀个令⼈担忧的临界点。除了 2023 年短暂升⾄ 5% 之外，上⼀次 10 年期国债收益率徘徊在
5% 以上，还是在全球⾦融危机爆发前⼣。
隔夜， 10 年期美债收益率盘中⼀度升⾄ 5.012% ，创 2007 年以来最⾼盘中⽔平，随后回落⾄
4.960% 收盘。这⼀关键⻔槛的触及，正迫使投资者直⾯⼀个核⼼问题：债券市场是否正在进⼊
⼀个全新时代？
市场当前存在两种截然对⽴的叙事。⼀种认为 5% 将如 2023 年 10 ⽉那般成为阶段性顶部，随后收

益率将重新回落；另⼀种则担忧收益率将决定性地突破这⼀⻔槛，复现 2000 年代乃⾄ 1990 年代
收益率⻓期⾼企的格局。答案将对消费者、企业乃⾄美国政府的借贷成本产⽣深远影响，并可
能左右即将到来的中期选举⾛向。
战争冲击叠加通胀压⼒，推动收益率触及关键⻔槛
此轮收益率上⾏的直接导⽕索，是中东局势持续升温引发的能源价格飙涨。布伦特原油上周⼤
涨近 9% ，此前伊朗⽀持的胡塞武装有效控制了也⻔西海岸另⼀处航运咽喉要道。截⾄周⼀，布
伦特原油⼩幅上涨 1% ，报每桶 105.68 美元。
能源价格上涨强化了市场对通胀持续⾼企的预期。周五公布的通胀数据偏强，令投资者⼏乎⼀
致预期美联储将在本周三的会议上加息，并在此后继续收紧货币政策。特朗普多次公开呼吁美
联储降息，令美联储主席沃什陷⼊两难境地，⽽市场对加息的预期却在持续攀升。
10 年期美债收益率是经济体内利率的关键驱动因素，其近期上⾏已将抵押贷款利率重新推升⾄
接近 7% 的⽔平。财政部⻓ Scott Bessent 此前已采取⾮常规措施试图压制收益率，但⽬前收效甚

微。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
两种历史情景，市场各执⼀词
周⼀盘中⾛势令部分投资者联想到 2023 年 10 ⽉ 23 ⽇的那⼀幕 —— 当时 10 年期收益率同样在早盘
触及 5% ，随后当⽇⼤幅回落⾄ 4.8% 上⽅，显⽰出投资者在等待这⼀⾥程碑被触及后便蜂拥买⼊
债券的典型⾏为。
然⽽，此次回调幅度明显不及 2023 年，这令不少投资者认为，收益率在未来数周乃⾄数⽉内轻
易突破 5% 并⾮没有可能。
PGIM Credit 联席⾸席投资官 Greg Peters 表⽰： " 我⼀直在问⾃⼰， ' 好，什么会成为利率下⾏的
催化剂？ '除了经济衰退，很难找到答案。当前的条件⾮常有利于收益率维持⾼位甚⾄进⼀步上
⾏。 "
另⼀⽅⾯，美国银⾏⾼级美国利率策略师 Meghan Swiber 则持不同看法： " 如果美联储本周加

息，并传递出将不惜⼀切⼿段控制通胀的信号，我们认为这实际上有助于压低⻓端利率。 "
债务规模与供给压⼒，构成收益率上⾏的结构性⽀撑
部分投资者认为，此轮收益率上⾏在⼀定程度上反映的是经济的正常化 —— 回归 2008 年⾦融危
机前、央⾏⼤规模购债与超低利率时代之前的状态。
但当前形势亦有其独特之处。美国联邦债务总额近期突破 40 万亿美元，较⼗年前翻了⼀番。债
务规模扩⼤意味着国债供给增加，潜在地压低债券价格、推⾼收益率。财政部在⻉森特主导下
近期已开始加⼤对⻓期债券的回购⼒度，但相对于国债总存量⽽⾔，这些购买规模仍然微乎其
微。
众议员 David Schweikert （共和党，亚利桑那州）周⼀在社交媒体上写道： " 今天的数字应该让
国会感到恐惧。 "
AI 热潮与股市繁荣，部分抵消⾼利率冲击
多位分析师指出，由 AI 投资热情驱动的股市繁荣，在⼀定程度上缓冲了⾼收益率对经济的冲
击。
AllianceBernstein ⾸席经济学家 Eric Winograd 表⽰： " 通常情况下，更⾼的收益率和借贷成本可

能会抑制企业投资。但许多科技公司现在将投资 AI 视为关乎⽣死存亡的战略选择。我认为它们对
⾦融条件的反应⽅式与其他企业截然不同。 "
这⼀因素使得市场对经济能否承受 5% 收益率⽽不迅速减速持相对乐观态度，也令 " 收益率将⻓期
维持⾼位 " 的叙事获得了更多⽀撑。
限免

美国以后⼤概率会出现国债收益率上涨，然后股市也上涨的，反直觉的现象。伴随实际通胀的增
⻓。美联储现在和⽇本央⾏是⼀样的，搞新货币政策，这种套路的爆炸点不在崩盘，⽽在于类似
于⽇本现在这样的，通胀和货币贬值。观察各种⼤型公司发债的情况，就看得出来了。

以及与其他投资品的收益⻛险⽐。更关键的是，名义收益率与实际收益率之间的关系。在美联储
执⾏新货币政策的背景下，美国的流动性本质上是⽆限的，只要它能够得到正向收益，如果赔本
的话，⼏个点就能把各⼤⾦融机构的那点核⼼资产赔光。

市場焦點
美國
• 美國經濟呈現「內需信心降溫、長端利率仍持高檔」的分歧訊號：ADP 8月EMC指數連4月下
滑，顯示員工工作信心與投入度走弱 ，若進一步影響消費 ，可能加大經濟降溫壓力；另一方
面，外國美債持有量下降 ，加上財政赤字與 AI企業融資需求推升長期資金供給壓力 ，使30年
期殖利率一度升至近 20年高點，即便美財部擴大長債回購規模 ，但此舉恐僅具短效 。整體而
言，美國景氣降溫與長端利率高檔並存 ，市場將更關注消費韌性 、財政政策與期限溢價變化 。
亞洲
• 過去兩個月 ，亞洲市場的去槓桿進程已大致完成 ，隨著第⼆季度財報季接近尾聲 ，市場關注
點可能重新轉向企業基本面 ，中國和韓國市場融資餘額已回落至 2025年⽔平，並開始重新成
長， 顯示此前離場觀望的資金正逐步回流 ，值得注意的是本輪資金回流更有選擇性 ，流向那

些已從全球AI基建熱潮中展現出獲利能力的企業，北亞半導體板塊後市持續看好。
日本
• 日本2026年第⼆季實質GDP季增0.3%，年化增速僅1.1%，均低於市場預期的 0.5%及2.0%。
儘管GDP低於預期 ，市場對日本央行持續推進貨幣政策正常化的預期仍未明顯改變 ，而升息
有利銀行等金融股擴大利差 。中長期而言 ，建議聚焦具結構性成長題材的產業 ，尤其AI需求
已由資料中心延伸至機器人 、自動化及電力設備 ，有望帶動相關設備與零組件需求持續成長 。

美企職場信心持續降溫、內需韌性面臨考驗
• 員工信心連4月走弱，勞動市場出現降溫訊號：ADP Research Institute公布8月員工動機與承諾指數（EMC Index）連
續第四個月下滑，並回落至2025年1月以來低點；19個產業中有12個走弱，其中住宿餐飲業跌幅擴大，其他個人服務業
亦創新低。知識型工作者情緒降至近一年半最低，顯示員工工作投入與信心持續降溫。
• 企業人力配置審慎，消費動能成後續觀察焦點：ADP數據反映職場信心轉弱，聯準會褐皮書亦指出多數地區就業大致持
平，企業招聘態度偏審慎；消費支出僅小幅增加 ，中低所得家庭對物價與能源成本更為敏感 。一旦勞動市場降溫，將影
響工作穩定度與所得預期，進而影響消費意願，使得內需隱然成為下半年美國景氣的重要觀察變數。

• 後市觀察： 若員工信心持續走弱並逐步傳導至消費 ，雖有提高美國經濟成長放緩的可能性 ，但亦有利於市場重新評估
Fed政策寬鬆空間；建議配置上可採股債均衡 ，債券以中短天期美債及投資級公司債為核心 ，受惠於經濟降溫下利率下
行潛力與較高票息收益；股票則可偏向醫療保健、必需消費等防禦型類股，以降低景氣與消費放緩的波動。
資料來源： ADP Research Worker Sentiment Survey， 2026.8.19

財政供給壓力升溫，長債殖利率維持高檔
• 海外美債持有量下降 ，長端供需壓力升高： 美國財政部 6月
TIC數據顯示，外國持有美國公債總額較前月減少 721億美元，
降至9.3兆美元，其中日本與中國分別減持 264億及259億美
元，前十大主要海外持債國有 8國持有量下降；雖然 6月美債
交易仍呈淨流入 ，但海外持有量下降顯示部分主要投資人的
配置意願轉趨審慎 ，疊加美國財政赤字擴大與發債供給增加 ，
長端債券供需壓力升高，令殖利率仍維持高檔。
• AI融資需求增加 ，長端資金競爭推升期限溢價： AI基礎建設
投資帶動大型科技企業增加長天期債券融資 ，與政府大量發
債共同提高長期資金需求；在短端利率受經濟降溫與政策預
期影響相對偏弱下 ，長端殖利率仍維持高檔 ，即便美國財政

部宣布擴大長債回購規模 ，然此舉恐僅具短效 ，殖利率曲線
改變陡峭化現象仍有待時間推移。
• 市場看法： 由於長端美債短期仍受財政供給 、海外需求及期
限溢價牽制 ，一旦後續經濟持續降溫 、通膨壓力回落 ，或財
政政策逐步收斂 ，長債殖利率或有望回落；建議投資人目前
可採股債均衡 、分批布局，債券以高品質投資級債及中短天
期美債穩定收益；股票則保留優質 AI科技龍頭 ，掌握AI資本
支出與企業獲利成長機會。

財政部版扭轉操作重磅登場：美國擴大長債回購解析與資產配置策略
• 政策動機與市場背景 -壓制高企殖利率，緩解財政與經濟壓力： 受中東地緣政治局勢 、通膨疑慮以及龐大財政赤字影響 ，全
球長端國債殖利率近期持續攀升 。美國30年期國債殖利率一度衝破5.3%大關，創下2007年以來的新高。殖利率大幅飆升不
僅加重了聯邦政府負擔高額利息的壓力 (財政部近期剛支付創紀錄 850億美元利息支出)，也推高房貸與企業借貸成本 ，為整
體經濟成長帶來阻礙。為捍衛金融市場穩定並降低融資成本，美國財政部長史考特·貝森特宣布自2026年9月9日起，將10年
至30年期名義國債的單次流動性支持回購上限提升至少一倍 (由20億美元提高至至少 40億美元)。市場預計此舉將使每季國
債回購規模增加約160億至280億美元，這標誌著財政部主動動用政策工具干預市場，發出「政策底線」已現的強烈訊號。

• 操作機制與市場影響：本次擴大回購主要針對流動性較差舊券，雖然財政部尚未明確說明資金來源，但市場普遍預期將增發
期限一年內的短期國庫券來籌集資金。此種買長債、發短債的做法，類似於聯準會過去實施「扭轉操作」，即藉由抽離市場
中的期間風險(Duration)，達到壓低長期殖利率目的。德意志銀行與花旗集團等外資機構指出 ，這種人為干預殖利率曲線的
做法屬於一種溫和的金融抑制，當美債殖利率無法自由反映市場風險時，外資持有美債的風險補償轉由匯率承擔，這將對美
元指數構成中長期下行壓力。多數華爾街機構認為，與每月高達2.25兆美元的國庫券發行總額相比，增加回購規模依然相對
偏小，只要聯準會貨幣政策尚未轉向降息且赤字與通膨基本面未變，單靠財政部回購操作難以徹底扭轉長端公債的拋售浪潮。
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026/8/20
過去聯準會扭轉操作降低殖利率
• 總結及建議：財政部擴大長債回購成功短暫遏制了殖利
率飆升的失控風險，並為債市劃定了明確的政策防線。
然而，在基本面通膨與財政赤字問題未解前，市場波動

性仍將維持在高檔 。鑑於財政部在 5.2%–5.3%區域展
現出明確的干預意願，長端殖利率進一步大幅暴漲空間
有限，投資人可佈局7至10年期債券，以獲取穩健的利
息回報；對於 20年至30年期超長債 ，則建議採取超跌
反彈的波段操作策略。

• AI資本支出承諾大增，財務槓桿隱憂升溫：Alphabet、Meta、微軟、亞馬遜等9家科技巨頭近期披露的「表外業務（尚未
正式列入公司資產負債表內的潛在債務）」合計約3兆美元，約為過去一年資本支出的5倍。隨著AI基礎建設投資快速擴張，
部分企業自由現金流已轉負，若未來AI需求或獲利不及預期，龐大的固定支出可能壓縮現金流與財務彈性。
• 聚焦信用品質，降低尾端風險：目前科技巨頭尚未面臨急迫的償債危機，但大量採購合約及表外業務，使企業實際信用風險
可能高於財報所呈現的槓桿⽔準。對債券投資而言，建議優先布局現金流穩健、資產負債表強健的公司債；同時審慎看待高
度依賴AI資本支出與結構性融資的企業債。若未來AI投資報酬率下滑，信用利差擴大將可能先於股票市場反映風險，因此債
券配置宜採取高品質、較短存續期間與分散產業曝險策略，以兼顧收益與防禦性。

經理人維持樂觀情緒，股票配置創近五年新高
• 市場情緒呈現極度樂觀，但風險意識同步抬頭的雙重特徵。根據8月份美銀美林經理人調查報告共識 —經濟不著陸、AI資本
支出不減、聯準會短線不升息也不降息。然而，資金大量湧入股市也讓籌碼出現高度擁擠現象，容錯率相對降低。
• 股票加碼創近 5年新高，現金水位降至歷史警戒線： 股票淨加碼由上月 42%飆升至淨56%加碼，創下2021年11月以來最高
⽔準(連14個月加碼)，債券配置則擴大至淨 39%減碼。法人最偏好新興股市 (淨34%加碼)與美股(淨27%加碼，創近20個月
新高)，產業方面則大幅加碼科技類股(由淨18%升至淨30%加碼)。經理人現金配置由上月3.6%進一步降至3.5%，為2026年
2月以來最低。法人操作上，現金⽔位低於4%通常被視為反向賣出/警戒訊號，意味著後續可加碼的閒置資金已相當有限。

• 最擁擠交易與尾端風險：做多全球半導體雖連續居最擁擠交易首位 ，但認同比例從7月創紀錄82%明顯降至53%，顯示資金
開始出現輪動 ，做空日圓與做多科技七雄分居⼆ 、三名。71%經理人預期雲端巨頭 2026年不會削減 AI資本支出 ，然而，
38%經理人將AI雲端巨頭資本支出評為最可能引發系統性信用事件來源 ，且認為企業資產負債表槓桿過高的比例由淨 7%飆
升至淨19%，顯示市場對企業融資需求與財務紀律高度關注。AI泡沫化連續第⼆個月居最大尾端風險，但佔比已由上月下滑，
債券殖利率無序攀升由第三名升至第⼆大風險，主因財政赤字與通膨黏性，而第⼆波通膨反映對原油與物價回升的警惕。
資料來源：美銀美林證券，富蘭克林投顧整理，2026.8

內需疲弱，日本經濟成長動能放緩
日本GDP年成長 日本利率走勢預估
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.8.11 資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.8.11
• 內需疲弱，日本經濟成長動能放緩：日本2026年第⼆季實質GDP季增0.3%，年化增速僅1.1%，均低於市場預期的0.5%及
2.0%。主要受到家庭消費與企業資本支出疲弱拖累，顯示生活成本上升仍壓抑消費意願，內需復甦力道尚待強化。不過，出
口表現仍具韌性，全球AI投資亦持續支撐日本汽車、半導體等產業需求，經濟並未出現失速跡象。
• 政策正常化延續，日股聚焦結構性成長：儘管GDP低於預期，市場對日本央行持續推進貨幣政策正常化的預期仍未明顯改變，
主要受通膨壓力及薪資趨勢支撐。對日股而言，升息有利銀行等金融股擴大利差，同時企業獲利與治理改革仍提供基本面支

撐。中長期而言，建議維持對日股的正向看法，並聚焦具結構性成長題材的產業，尤其AI需求已由資料中心延伸至機器人、
自動化及電力設備，有望帶動相關設備與零組件需求持續成長；因此，日股仍具配置價值，可優先布局具技術優勢、獲利能
見度高的企業。

8月中國貸款市場報價利率繼續持穩
• 8月中國貸款市場報價利率繼續持穩 ：主因有⼆，首先是定價基礎未出現變化 ，自上月LPR公布以來，主要政策利率(如央行7
天期逆回購利率)維持穩定，使LPR報價缺乏調整依據 。其次是銀行端下調LPR的動能不足，AAA級同業存單(最安全大型銀行
發出來的短期借款工具)也維持基本穩定，這意味著商業銀行近期在貨幣市場的批發融資成本變化不大 ，同時第⼆季商業銀行
淨息差雖回升至1.41%，但還是在歷史低點，使銀行更傾向維持利差，因此主動降息意願降低。
• 展望後市：7月底召開的中央政治局會議，重申了放鬆貨幣政策立場， 特別是7月中國經濟數據凸顯了進一步放鬆貨幣政策的
必要性-工業增加值年增率從6月份5.3%放緩至4.5%，社會消費品零售總額增速從6月份的1.0%放緩至0.6%；固定資產投資降

幅進一步擴大，彭博表示人行將在9月或10月將關鍵政策利率下調10個基點的預期不變，LPR隨後預計也將隨之調降，以支撐
消費與投資並對沖外部風險。
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.8.20

地緣政治風險持續干擾全球能源供給
• 中東石油供應受阻，致力尋求替代出口路線：美伊衝突持續，荷姆茲海峽通航受阻，石油出口受影響最嚴重的國家包括沙烏
地阿拉伯、伊拉克、科威特及阿聯酋。各國正致力尋找出口替代方案，沙國透過東西向管線改由紅海出口原油，伊拉克則透
過土耳其傑伊漢港等替代路線出口。截至7月，OPEC+產量已從低點回升約330萬桶/日，但仍較戰前低約550萬桶/日。
• 油品市場比原油市場更緊張：目前中東精煉油品出口量僅恢復至戰前約一半⽔平，此外，由於烏克蘭攻擊俄羅斯煉油設施，
衝擊俄羅斯柴油生產，俄羅斯政府於7月初宣布柴油出口禁令(佔全球柴油出口10%)，令能源通膨壓力進一步升溫。
• 若中東通航問題解決 ，彭博預估能源供給將快速轉為過剩： 彭博預估，一旦荷姆茲海峽重新開放 ，超過80%的受影響供應

量可望在6個月內恢復，全球石油市場將由目前供應不足迅速轉為供給過剩，國際油價有望回落至每桶60美元附近。
• 觀點：油價或不至於衝高至衝突初期的每桶 120美元，但短期維持高檔震盪的可能性仍高 ，可適度配置能源類股、煉油股來
對沖通膨風險。
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.8.20。

息日前一日之淨值×100%。

从劳动投入和薪资来看，企业用工需求边际增强，中期薪资放缓趋势仍未
改变，但环比增速有所回升，显示薪资增长出现边际修复。 8 月私人非农
企业平均每周工作时长由 34.3 小时升至 34.4 小时，显示企业劳动投入有
所增加。平均时薪同比增长 3.09%，低于前值 3.24%以及过去 12 个月平
均水平 3.64%，中期薪资增速放缓趋势延续；但环比增长 0.27%，较前值
有所回升，显示薪资增长出现短期修复。8 月就业数据公布后，市场重新
评估美联储政策路径，就业市场表现强于预期，显示美国经济仍具一定韧
性，加息预期有所升温。 
8 月就业数据落地，叠加沃什表态美国已接近充分就业、政策判断主要看
通胀，市场重新评估加息路径 。美股小幅走弱，美债收益率抬升，加息预
期边际升温。本次就业数据反映美国劳动力市场整体仍具韧性，但行业分
化与结构性压力持续存在。沃什此前表示，当前美国劳动力市场整体仍接
近充分就业，政策判断重点将更多取决于通胀回落进程。后续市场仍需关
注 8 月 CPI 数据、FOMC 政策信号，以及 AI 基础设施投资周期对制造
业、公用事业及相关行业就业需求的支撑是否具备持续性。

风险提示：美国劳动力市场上行超预期，美国通胀中枢居高不下且高通胀
存续时长超预期，美国货币财政政策超预期。

### Page 5

宏观经济点评  
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 5 
1. 8 月非农表现强劲，但季节性因素与结构性隐忧仍存 
1.1 非农表现强劲，但就业增长持续性仍待验证 
8 月非农就业表现强于预期，但新增就业主要集中于季节性较强行业，持续性仍需
观察。8 月美国非农就业人数增加 16.2 万人，大幅高于市场预期的 5.6 万人，也高于过
去 12 个月平均的 3.52 万人。与此同时，前期就业数据有所上修：6 月非农新增就业人
数由 2 万人上修至 3.1 万人，7 月由减少 2.3 万人上修至增加 2.1 万人，6 月和 7 月合计
较此前增加 5.5 万人。其中餐饮服务业和地方政府教育是本月就业增长的主要贡献来源，
两者合计增加 10.11 万人。不过，由于这两个行业均存在较强的季节性因素，当前增长
是否具备持续性仍有待后续数据验证。 8 月 U3 失业率维持在 4.1%，劳动参与率则由
61.4%升至 61.6%， 显示更多劳动力重新进入就业市场。 在此背景下，U6 广义失业率下
降至 7.7%， 表明就业市场边际有所改善， 就业质量或有所提升。非农数据公布后，CME
市场定价显示加息预期有所升温，9 月会议加息 25 个基点的概率升至 58.4%；12 月会
议加息一次的概率小幅下降，而加息两次的概率上升幅度更大，强劲的就业数据降低了
市场对经济放缓的担忧，并提高了美联储进一步收紧货币政策的 可能性。 
1.2 地方政府教育支撑非农增长， 但季节性调整影响与趋势变化仍需关注 
地方政府教育就业对 8 月非农形成支撑，但其持续性仍需观察，当前数据或同时受
到季节性调整和基本面变化影响。政府就业 8 月增加 3.5 万人，明显高于前值减少 5 万
人， 主要贡献来自地方政府教育， 该行业就业增加4.19 万人。 同时， 上月数据上修0.97
万人， 基本抵消了此前的下降。一方面，地方政府教育就业的季节性修复规律仍然存在，
但今年的回升幅度可能弱于历史水平。 地方政府教育就业通常在 7 月达到低点，随后随
着新学年开始逐步回升，主要受到暑期临时合同结束以及学校重新开学等季节性因素影
响。然而，今年 7 月就业低点进一步下探，同时后续高点的回升幅度也有所减弱，显示
该行业就业恢复力度可能不及往年。另一方面，需要注意季节调整模型在面对非季节性
重大冲击时可能产生一定偏差。 由于季节调整主要依据历史同期数据估计季节性模式，
异常观测可能会影响季节因子的计算， 从而导致季调数据出现短期波动1。 疫情前地方政
府教育就业的季调序列相对平滑， 说明模型能够较好捕捉稳定的季节性变化； 但疫情后，
7、8 月份季调数据波动明显加大，反映出在结构性冲击下， 历史季节规律对当前数据的
解释能力可能下降。当前，教育财政补贴退坡或成为新的非季节性冲击因素，并可能进
一步放大季节调整带来的短期波动。因此，8 月地方政府教育就业的增长，需要结合后
续数据观察，以判断其主要来自季节调整因素修正，还是行业就业需求的实际变化。  
1.3 商品与服务业同步改善，行业内部冷热不均 
私人部门就业方面，商品和服务业均实现增长。其中，商品部门新增 4.1 万人，高
于前值 2.9 万人；服务业新增 8.6 万人，高于前值 4.2 万人，二者均高于过去 12 个月平
均水平，显示私人就业整体有所改善。商品部门就业增长主要来自建筑业和制造业，延
续了上月改善趋势， 但内部结构有所变化。建筑业方面， 非住宅建筑仍是主要支撑力量，
但住宅相关就业本月明显回升。住宅建筑业在 2024 年高位盘整后持续下降，本月增加
0.73 万人； 住宅专业承包商也增加0.34 万人。 房地产相关就业占建筑业新增就业的48%，
较此前疲弱表现有所改善。不过，在高利率环境下，住宅市场恢复的持续性仍需进一步
观察。此外，根据美国住房与城市发展部和人口普查局数据，7 月美国住房开工量下降
12.4%，经季节调整后的年化率为 124 万套，经济不确定性、建筑成本上涨、劳动力短
缺以及融资成本较高仍对住宅建设形成压力2。制造业从 2025 年 12 月低点以来累计增
加 5.8 万人。本月增长主要集中于耐用品制造业，制造业新增 1.6 万人，其中耐用品制
造贡献 1.5 万人。细分来看，金属制品制造增加 0.57 万人，机械制造增加 0.61 万人，
资本品相关产业链仍保持一定韧性。不过，运输设备制造业减少 0.1 万人，其中机动车

### Page 6

宏观经济点评  
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 6 
及零部件减少 0.45 万人，汽车产业链就业再次走弱，成为耐用品制造内部的主要拖累。 
服务业就业增长动能有所恢复，但内部表现并不均衡 ，主要呈现了三个特点，第一
是休闲和住宿业成为 8 月服务业就业增长的主要来源， 但本轮反弹或主要依赖餐饮业修
复，行业需求仍未明显改善，持续性仍需观察 。休闲和住宿业在连续两个月下降后明显
反弹，本月增加 6.2 万人，占私人部门新增就业的 48.8%。其中，餐饮服务业和饮酒场
所新增 5.92 万人，为 2023 年 1 月以来最大单月增幅。而艺术、娱乐和休闲行业就业继
续下降，显示休闲和住宿业内部复苏并不均衡。同时，考虑到该行业此前连续下滑，本
次增长或包含一定回补因素，且餐饮就业月度波动较大，后续数据可能存在修正。美国
餐饮协会数据显示，虽然餐饮业就业仍处于温和扩张阶段，但消费者客流仍不稳定， 过
去 18 个月中，7 月是第 17 个月出现经营者反馈客流净下降的月份，显示消费者需求仍
未完全恢复，未来经营者在招聘方面可能保持谨慎3。第二是， 医疗和专业服务短期增速
放缓， 但长期结构性需求仍提供支撑。医疗保健和社会援助行业新增 2.84 万人， 其中医
疗保健行业增加 1.29 万人，连续 4 个月低于过去 12 个月平均水平，主要增长来自家庭
医疗服务。社会援助行业增加 1.55 万人，高于过去 12 个月平均水平，其中个人和家庭
服务贡献 1.28 万人。尽管近期增速有所放缓，但根据美国劳工统计局最新 2025—2035
年就业预测，受人口老龄化和慢性疾病增加推动，医疗保健和社会援助行业仍预计将成
为未来十年新增就业最多的行业。专业、科学和技术服务业新增 1 万人，其中管理、科
学和技术咨询服务增加 0.48 万人，临时帮助服务增加 0.68 万人。根据美国劳工统计局
的预测，随着人工智能系统应用、研发活动以及相关咨询需求增长，该行业就业仍具有
一定支撑。第三是高薪行业就业压力仍未明显缓解 。金融业本月减少 1.1 万人，与上月
降幅持平；信息业减少 2.3 万人，在上月短暂回升后重新进入下降区间。其中，信息技
术相关六个子行业均出现下降，出版业以及计算机基础设施、数据处理、网络托管及相
关服务这两个子行业的降幅较为明显，分别减少 0.67 万人和 0.77 万人。金融行业中两
大子行业也均下滑，金融保险业减少 0.74 万人，房地产及租赁业减少 0.35 万人。 
1.4 劳动参与率回升的同时就业压力缓解，但长期失业风险仍存 
8 月劳动力市场表现出较强韧性，新增劳动力主要被就业市场吸收，广义就业压力
持续缓解，但长期失业占比上升仍需关注。8 月 U3 失业率维持在 4.1%，但劳动参与率
由 61.4%升至 61.6%，显示更多劳动力重新进入就业市场。从年龄结构来看，劳动参与
率的上升主要集中在 24 岁以下和 55 岁以上人群， 而25-54 岁核心劳动年龄人口参与率
仍维持在 83.4%的高位，后续上升趋势的持续性仍需观察。从劳动力规模来看，劳动力
人口增加 68.3 万人，其中就业人口增加 56.9 万人，失业人口增加 11.5 万人。新增劳动
者大部分被就业市场吸收，仅少部分进入失业状态，因此失业率维持稳定，表明当前就
业市场仍具备一定吸纳能力， 整体表现相对健康。从广义就业压力来看，U6 失业率进一
步下降， 由7.9%降至 7.7%，U6 与 U3 之间的差距继续收窄， 表明边缘劳动力以及因经
济原因从事兼职就业的人群均有所减少，劳动力闲置压力持续缓解。 其中，边缘劳动力
占非劳动力人口比例由去年同期的 1.74%下降至 1.57%， 显示潜在就业人口重新进入就
业市场的压力有所改善；因经济原因兼职就业人数明显下降，环比减少 8.48%，占兼职
就业比例由 17.51%下降至 16.3%，显示被迫兼职压力有所减弱。从失业结构来看，劳
动力市场流动性有所改善，但长期失业压力仍需关注。 从失业原因来看，临时裁员占比
由 13.2%下降至 12.0%，暗示部分此前暂时失业人员重新回归岗位，也可能解释了因经
济原因兼职人数下降。主动离职者占比由 11.3%升至 13.2%，显示劳动者对就业机会的
信心有所增强； 新进入劳动力市场者占比则由10.9%下降至 10%。 从失业持续时间来看，
少于 5 周、5-14 周以及 14-26 周失业者占比均有所下降，仅 27 周以上长期失业者占比
由 25.5%升至 27%，表明短期就业压力有所缓解，但长期失业群体仍面临一定压力。 
1.5 劳动投入边际增强，薪资增速放缓趋势未改

### Page 7

宏观经济点评  
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 7 
整体来看，8 月劳动力市场韧性仍存， 劳动投入和薪资增速均出现边际改善。8 月私
人非农企业员工平均每周工作时长在连续四个月维持 34.3 小时后， 小幅升至34.4 小时，
显示企业劳动需求仍具一定韧性。 从行业来看，商品生产和服务业平均工作时长均上升
0.1 小时，其中制造业平均工作时长升至 40.5 小时，但加班时间仍维持在 3.1 小时，表
明企业更多是通过延长正常工作时间应对需求， 而非明显增加加班安排。 细分行业来看，
工作时长增幅主要集中在耐用品制造、 公用事业以及金融服务业， 或反映出AI 产业扩张
对资本品、 电力基础设施及相关服务需求的带动， 显示AI 投资周期仍对部分行业形成支
撑。名义时薪同比增速为 3.09%， 低于前值3.24%， 也低于过去12 个月平均水平 3.64%；
但环比增速为 0.27%，高于前值 0.16%，与过去 12 个月均值基本持平。整体来看，中
期薪资增速放缓趋势并未改变，但单月数据显示薪资增长出现边际修复，后续仍需结合
更多月份数据判断其持续性。从近三个月平均时薪环比增速来看，金融业和建筑业薪资
增长相对较快。不过，金融业同期裁员人数有所增加，同时平均工作时长上升，薪资增
长或更多来自低薪岗位退出后的结构性影响， 以及企业对核心人才留任带来的薪资溢价，
并不一定代表行业需求全面扩张。 相比之下， 建筑业就业人数增加， 且受益于AI 基础设
施建设相关需求，薪资上升或更可能体现为需求拉动型增长。 
1.6 就业韧性强化加息预期，通胀仍是政策关键约束  
8 月就业数据公布后，市场重新评估加息路径，后续走势仍取决于通胀回落进程与
政策信号。数据公布后，美股三大指数小幅下行，纳斯达克综合指数下跌 0.29%，标普
500 指数下跌 0.38%，道琼斯工业平均指数下跌 0.51%。避险资产方面，COMEX 黄金
下跌 1.38%。 美债收益率曲线呈现熊平走势，10 年期美债收益率上行 1.8bp 至 7.784%，
2 年期美债收益率上行 2.8bp 至 4.366%。这一市场反应也与沃什此前对就业与通胀形
势的判断相呼应，即当前就业市场并未出现明显失速，政策约束更多来自通胀回落的不
确定性。沃什在杰克逊霍尔会议上表示，当前劳动力市场较低的人员流动率，部分源于
疫情后劳动力市场经历的大规模雇主与雇员重新匹配。 在劳动力供给增长有限的情况下，
新增就业岗位自然会有所减少。虽然部分群体（例如应届毕业生）的就业情况仍值得关
注，但整体来看，大多数希望就业的人仍能够维持工作或找到工作，当前劳动力市场状
况仍基本符合充分就业的标准。不过，在价格稳定目标方面，沃什认为当前通胀压力更
值得关注。美联储关注的 PCE 价格指数 12 个月和 6 个月变化率分别为 3.7%和 4.1%，
CPI 对应指标同样处于较高水平， 核心通胀指标也显示通胀仍高于2%的目标。 因此， 沃
什认为，美联储当前政策重点应更多关注通胀风险。后续来看，市场仍需重点关注 8 月
CPI 数据、FOMC 会议释放的利率政策信号， 以及AI 基础设施投资周期对制造业、 公用
事业及相关行业就业需求的支撑是否具备持续性。 
2. 风险提示 
美国劳动力市场上行超预期，美国通胀中枢居高不下且高通胀存续时长超预期，美
国货币财政政策超预期。

投资建议的评级标准  评级 说明 
评级标准为报告发布日后的 6 个月内公司股价 （或行业
指数） 相对同期基准指数的相对市场表现。 其中A 股市
场以沪深 300 指数为基准； 新三板市场以三板成指 （针
对协议转让标的） 或三板做市指数 （针对做市转让标的）
为基准；香港市场以恒生指数为基准。 
股票评级 
买入 相对同期基准指数涨幅在 10%以上 
增持 相对同期基准指数涨幅在 5%~10%之间 
中性 相对同期基准指数涨幅在-5%~+5%之间 
减持 相对同期基准指数跌幅在 5%以上 
行业评级 
看好 相对表现优于市场 
中性 相对表现与市场持平 
看淡 相对表现弱于市场

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Moderate Growth, Broadening Opportunities
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
Although the Citi Economic Surprise Index has been easing over the past month as a
rising number of data releases have fallen short of elevated expectations (Exhibit 1), the
data remains broadly positive and consistent with sustained moderate growth. Indeed,
with alternating waves of headwinds and tailwinds, real consumer spending and gross

domestic product (GDP) continue to expand near their longer-run average rates even
with substantial wealth effects and strong growth in government transfer payments.
Employment and hours worked have barely increased over the past year, leaving
productivity to account for nearly all the economic growth. Combined with moderating
wage growth, this has contained unit labor costs—a positive for inflation dynamics.
Despite stubborn readings above the Federal Reserve's (Fed) 2% target inflation through

July, consumer price index data were relatively favorable, and although the latest personal
consumption expenditures (PCE) report was less reassuring, much of the upside pressure
remained concentrated in healthcare, tariff- and energy-sensitive categories, as well as
in volatile financial services that tend to follow equity-market gains. The latter inflation
category is slated to be revised lower when new index methodologies are announced
next month. Meanwhile, a broad range of other goods and services showed signs of

cooling inflation on a three-month annualized basis, as shown in Exhibit 2.
Exhibit 1: Economic Data Surprises Have Been More Mixed Over The Past Month…

Citi Economic Surprise Index: U.S.
Source: Bloomberg. Data as of August 26, 2026. It is not possible to invest directly in an index. Please refer to index
definitions at the end of this report. Past performance is no guarantee of future results.

Basically, tariffs, elevated oil prices, wealth effects and AI-related electronics price
inflation continue to put upward pressure on inflation and interest rates. Although higher
gasoline prices have largely offset the One Big Beautiful Bill Act’s (OBBBA) direct benefit
to consumers, the shift from a negative fiscal impulse in 2025 to a positive impulse this
year has supported aggregate demand, contributing to the stalled progress on headline
inflation. Government transfers increased rapidly in July to a new high as a share of

personal income, as aging baby boomers retire.
Upside pressures on inflation have been counteracted in large part by slowing wage
growth, subdued housing activity, relatively tame credit growth, strong productivity, and
soft unit labor cost increases. In our view, if demand materially exceeded productive
capacity, inflation reacceleration would be much more pervasive.
Also important, the effective tariff rate is expected to average approximately 10%

this year, broadly matching its 2025 average absent another policy shock. The tariff
contribution to inflation is thus expected to continue to fade from its estimated
early-2026 peak of roughly 0.8 percentage point. Further oil-price moderation and
methodological changes affecting financial-service prices and other categories seen
as misrepresenting true inflation will also help improve the picture. Alongside anchored
longer-term inflation expectations, this suggests a gradual further moderation in

inflation, which would improve household purchasing power and support economic

expansion with moderate inflation
as well as broadening earnings
growth across sectors and market

3 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
growth. Real consumer spending appears on track for approximately 2.5% to 3% annualized
growth in Q3, following + 0.5% in Q1 and +3.5% in Q2.
Also positive, business investment in equipment and intellectual property accelerated strongly
in the first half. Business investment in manufactured goods is particularly positive for
broadening economic growth given manufacturing activity’s high multiplier effect on jobs and

aggregate demand. Helped by surging AI investment, OBBBA tax incentives, and rebounding
profitability, manufacturing activity is rapidly improving after several weak years, helping
sustain real GDP growth near 2.1% year-over-year, in line with its 25-year average. Although
July “core” capital-goods orders were disappointing, June was revised substantially higher,
raising estimates for Q3 business investment. Strong regional manufacturing surveys also

point to strong momentum, mostly the direct result of surging AI-related investment.
While there is no single accepted estimate for AI investment in the U.S., it is generally
projected at around $600 billion in 2026, $850 billion in 2027, $950 billion in 2028, and over
$1 trillion in 2029. While reaching new highs, its growth rate—and therefore its contribution
to GDP growth—is eventually projected to moderate from +50% to +60% in 2026 and 2027
to +10% by 2029. Also important, its domestic impact is smaller than the headline total

suggests. According to industry estimates, 70% of AI spending is directed toward equipment,
most of it imported. Thus, only around 50% of projected U.S. AI investment is poised to
directly help the domestic economy—though still enough to help broaden growth through
expanding domestic data-center construction, equipment production, power generation, and
supporting infrastructure.
The manufacturing sector’s broader spillovers to suppliers, construction, transportation, and

services make the sector’s recovery an important support for continued expansion. Consistent
with surging AI capital expenditures, rebounding industrial production, and very strong profit
estimates both here and abroad, BofA’s Global Wave 1 indicator has continued to increase—a
typically favorable indicator for cyclical assets’ performance relative to defensives.
With the growth and inflation outlook remaining positive, concerns have shifted to fiscal

strains and uncertainty, as the 2026 deficit appears likely to exceed the Congressional Budget
Office’s February projection by about $200 billion, and publicly-held debt is projected to
rise from approximately 101% of GDP in 2026 to 120% by 2036—a path that could test
investor confidence.
However, since there is no identifiable debt threshold that automatically triggers a crisis,
and comparisons with smaller or externally indebted economies are imperfect, the loss of

confidence in the sustainability of the debt has been short-lived. Indeed, U.S. debt is dollar-
denominated and approximately 70% is domestically held. Deep capital markets and the dollar’s
global reserve currency role sustain structural Treasury demand. Also important, healthy private
sector balance sheets and productivity growth leave scope for gradual fiscal consolidation in
a solidly growing economy without the dire negative effects on the economy observed in past

debt crises overseas. Thus, despite recent concerns, Treasury yields remain within their three-
year range and broadly consistent with moderate growth and anchored inflation expectations,
their recent variations largely tracking energy prices and geopolitical developments.
Also important, the term premium has returned to pre-global-financial-crisis levels following
exceptionally low levels in the post-2008-2009 decade. However, its increase appears to

be just a normalization in a delayed response to previous monetary tightening. Indeed, the
estimated 10-year term premium—or compensation required by investors to hold long-term
debt rather than roll over short-term debt—has historically followed the federal funds rate
with a substantial lag of almost 3 years.
While government debt supply, central-bank balance sheet policy, inflation, and interest-rate
volatility shape the term premium, the lagged fed funds–term premium correlation suggests

that the 2024 to 2025 policy easing is likely to act as a moderating force on the term premium
for the foreseeable future. In our view, the growth and interest rate environment is, therefore,
likely to remain constructive for continued expansion and cyclical assets’ performance,
including materials and infrastructure investments while fiscal uncertainty, soft labor demand,
and an eventually moderating AI investment impulse argue for portfolio diversification.

1 BofA Global Wave quantifies global economic trends, and it is used to forecast stock market performance.

4 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Taking Stock of the End of Capital Abundance
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
For much of the past quarter century, the global economy enjoyed a remarkable luxury:
Capital was abundant, cheap and seemingly limitless.
Savings flowed freely across borders. Global interest rates drifted steadily lower and,
for a time, actually turned negative across parts of Europe and Japan. Governments

borrowed with few apparent consequences. Corporations financed growth at historically
attractive rates. Households gained access to inexpensive credit. And investors prospered
as declining discount rates helped push the valuations of stocks, bonds, real estate and
other financial assets higher.
Few countries benefited more from this age of capital abundance than the U.S. As the
world's largest debtor nation and home to the world's deepest capital markets, America

tapped the global savings pool with extraordinary ease. Foreign capital helped finance
persistent federal budget and current account deficits while keeping U.S. borrowing costs
remarkably low. Washington could spend more than it collected, households could consume
more than they earned, and the country as a whole could invest more than it saved.
For decades, the world was happy to finance the difference. That comfortable
arrangement is now being challenged. At precisely the moment when America's financing

needs have rarely been greater, the age of abundant capital is giving way to something
very different: The age of capital competition.
Importantly, the world is not running out of savings. According to the International
Monetary Fund, gross global savings remain around 27% of world GDP , roughly in line
with the average of this century. The supply of savings has not collapsed.
What has changed is the other side of the equation. The world has developed an

enormous appetite for capital. Governments, corporations and households are increasingly
competing for essentially the same pool of savings. And when demand for something
rises faster than its supply, its price tends to rise. For capital, that price is the interest rate.
The past was different. The previous era of abundance was unusual not merely because
the world saved a great deal but because investment demand was comparatively weak.
China, Germany, Japan, oil-producing nations and other surplus economies generated

enormous pools of excess savings. Emerging market central banks accumulated foreign
exchange reserves, much of which found its way into U.S. Treasury securities. Meanwhile,
following the global financial crisis, private investment across much of the developed
world remained subdued.
The upshot: Too much capital was chasing too few attractive and potential investment
opportunities.
Today, that equation is reversing. Global savings remain plentiful, but the list of claimants

on that capital is exploding. We call them the Five Ds: Debt, Defense, Digitalization,
Demographics and Deglobalization.
Start with debt. Governments across the developed world are running large structural
deficits while simultaneously confronting rising interest expenses. The U.S. stands
out. Federal debt held by the public is approaching the size of annual U.S. GDP , and
Washington must finance large new deficits while continually refinancing trillions of

dollars of existing obligations (Exhibits 3 and 4). Treasurys are therefore competing for
capital on a massive scale.
Then comes defense. Russia's invasion of Ukraine, tensions in the Middle East and
strategic competition between the U.S. and China have ended the post-Cold War peace
dividend. America is rebuilding munitions inventories, Europe is rearming, and Asian
Investment Implications
The age of capital competition will likely
favor quality companies with strong

balance sheets, durable cash flows and
capital discipline, in our view. Investors
should consider from the global
capital spending cycle via exposure to
themes such as power and electrical
infrastructure, defense, robotics,
semiconductors and data centers.

### Page 5

5 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
nations are increasing military expenditures. Ships, missiles, satellites, drones and
defense-production facilities all require capital.
Third is digitalization. The AI revolution may prove to be among the most capital-
intensive technological transformations in history. Technology companies are spending
extraordinary sums on data centers, advanced semiconductors, networking equipment

and power generation. Utilities need capital to expand and modernize electrical grids.
Semiconductor manufacturers require tens of billions of dollars to build advanced
fabrication plants. All the above is enormously expensive and demands capital.
Fourth is demographics. Aging populations are placing growing pressure on
government budgets through pensions, healthcare and other social programs. Fewer
workers relative to retirees mean greater fiscal burdens across much of the developed

world. In the U.S. alone, mandatory spending outlays on Medicaid, Medicare and Social
Security have jumped from $724 billion in 2000 to $3.2 trillion in FY 2025.
Finally, there is deglobalization. For decades, globalization was fundamentally about
efficiency: Produce goods wherever costs were lowest. Today's world increasingly prizes
resilience and national security, which means the duplication of supply chains, reshored
factories, expanding strategic inventories and subsidized domestic manufacturing

facilities. All these objectives, of course, require capital.
Indeed, the common denominator of debt, defense, digitalization, demographics and
deglobalization is access to capital. And since these factors are structural rather than
cyclical, their demands on global savings are unlikely to fade any time soon.
Evidence of this emerging competition is increasingly visible in financial markets.
Investment-grade companies have already sold nearly $1.5 trillion of bonds this year,

roughly 36% more than during the comparable period last year and on pace to challenge
previous records, according to Bloomberg. With hyperscalers expected to continue
pouring capital into AI infrastructure through the end of the decade, corporate demand
for financing could remain elevated for years.
Meanwhile, the U.S. Treasury must finance enormous federal deficits. The result is an
increasingly crowded marketplace in which governments and corporations are competing

for the same investor dollars. Recent shifts by investment grade bond funds away from
Treasurys and toward corporate debt offer a small but telling glimpse of what this new
environment may look like (Exhibits 5 and 6).
For investors, the implications are significant. First, real interest rates may remain
structurally higher than during much of the previous two decades. Investors waiting for a
return to the ultra-low-rate environment of the 2010s may be waiting a very long time.

Second, fiscal policy matters again. In an era of abundant capital, markets often tolerated
fiscal excess. In a world of greater capital competition, countries that borrow heavily
without generating corresponding economic returns may eventually pay a higher price.
Keep a close eye on Japan.
Third, capital allocation matters more. Companies capable of generating strong returns
on invested capital, maintaining healthy balance sheets and funding expansion internally

should command a premium. When money is no longer virtually free, the difference
between productive investment and financial engineering becomes much more important.
Fourth, the world's enormous capital spending cycle should create winners. Power
generation, electrical infrastructure, defense, industrial automation, semiconductors, data
centers and other capital-intensive industries could enjoy years of elevated investment
and higher-than-expected earnings growth, in our view.

In the end, the age of capital abundance is fading—the era whereby capital basically
chased borrowers is past. Prologue will be defined by borrowers chasing capital. The tide
has turned.

6 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What portfolio addition could potentially help mitigate a sharp rise in inflation and/or yields?
How about the start of a new hiking cycle where short-term interest rates rise consistently?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Officer
• If inflation or a weaker U.S. dollar are the reason for a sharp rise in yields, then
increasing exposure to Commodities as a complement to a core portfolio makes sense.

Above-average inflation that is consistent and not excessive mixed with a weaker
U.S. dollar are generally tailwinds for commodities and the companies that mine for
them. Additionally, if global economic growth is running above trend and the U.S. dollar
weakens as it normalizes relative to other currencies, then adding non-U.S Developed
Markets and Emerging Markets (EM) exposure also makes sense.
• If short rates rise sharply due to a new interest rate hiking cycle, a more defensive tilt

in asset exposure makes sense. In other words, in the equity markets more defensive
sectors become more in demand, lower-volatility and higher-quality areas become
more in demand, cyclical shares tend to feel downward pressure, and highly levered
areas without a ton of debt are generally impacted the most. If the short-term rate
rise is minimal and a new hiking cycle is not beginning, then a solid mixture of Value
and Growth Equities, laddered portfolio in bonds, exposure to commodities and

thematic ideas make the most sense overall, in our view.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

Personal Consumption Expenditures Price Index is a key economic measure that tracks the average change in prices paid by U.S. consumers for goods and services.
Citi Economic Surprise Index is an economic indicator that measures whether published macroeconomic data is beating or missing market expectations.
Consumer Price Index is a measure of the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a representative market basket of consumer goods and services.

investment advisory services, including the type of advice and assistance provided, the fees charged, and the rights and obligations of the parties. It is important to understand the differences,
particularly when determining which service or services to select. For more information about these services and their differences, speak with your Merrill financial advisor.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

September 03, 2026 
— As the summer holidays come to an end, we take a look at the three questions that are 
likely to occupy our time in the months ahead: Is the euro area economy proving 
resilient to the energy shock? Will the ECB stop at 2.50% or continue hiking until policy 
is restrictive? And will public finances and elections prove a destabilising 
combination?  
— The European economy is proving remarkably resilient, with some elements of 
positive demand shock offsetting the negative energy supply shock. Expectations are 
improving, but some caution is still needed: the energy shock is not over and the AI 
trade deficit is widening.  
— With respect to the ECB, a rapid normalisation of energy prices may be needed to stop 
the hiking cycle at 2.50%. That said, the inflation outlook is not yet consistent with an 
outright restrictive stance either.  
— Budget season is about to begin, debt dynamics are vulnerable and elections imply 
political risk and market volatility. The degree of tightening of financial conditions will 
be a function of political choices, the resilience (or not) of growth and the 
restrictiveness (or not) of ECB policy.

Q1: Is the euro area economy proving resilient to the energy shock? 
Strictly speaking, as a net energy importer the euro area should be highly exposed to the 
negative energy supply shock triggered by the Iran war. In any case, HICP inflation jumped 
to 3.0% in Q2, 1.2pp higher than expected at the start of the year. The household real 
income shock alone should have knocked more than 0.5pp off GDP .  
And yet euro area GDP was not weak in Q2, it was surprisingly strong. At 0.44% qoq, GDP 
growth exceeded expectations. We don’t have detailed components yet, but on the basis 
of available data household consumption grew despite the inflation shock.  
It is possible that elevated household savings buffered the real income shock on the 
assumption that it would be fleeting. There was also a targeted and temporary easing of 
fiscal policy (lower energy taxes), but this was not substantial and only moderated the rise 
in inflation slightly. That said, the euro area fiscal stance was already easy for 2026. Not 
nearly as easy as 2022, and skewed heavily towards Germany. But the stance is easy, not 
tight.  
Most encouraging are the signals of rising investment spending: the BLS points to demand 
for loans for fixed asset investment, the flow of long-term loans to corporates has been a 
little volatile but hints at investment spending, and the rise in German industrial orders 
from elsewhere in the euro area has historically been consistent with a euro area 
investment cycle too.

We need to be careful. We might be seeing the lagged transmission of the earlier 
ECB rate cuts. Private sector balance sheets are healthy, which would aid 
transmission. Maybe structural demand from AI investment  and defence 
spending is offsetting higher energy costs and geopolitical uncertainty.  
In short, maybe what Europe is facing is a positive demand story, not just a 
negative supply story. Having quickly dropped to 48.8 in April, the composite PMI 
rose to 52.1 in August and might have been higher were it not for heat -related 
headwinds in services. 
That said, there are reasons to be cautious about growth.  
The energy shock is not over. Gas prices have reached a new high for the year, 
raising the prospect of an electricity -based production cost shock to add to the 
existing oil-based transport cost shock.  
The energy shock might also be masked by temporary factors like stock-building. 
The shock in the Middle East went beyond energy into chemicals and metals. 
There was a threat to global supply chains. Fear of disruption might have 
triggered stock-building, which might explain why manufacturing has held up.  
There is no evidence of domestic inventory building. But Europe might have 
benefited from temporary stock -building in its export partners.  This might help 
explain export resilience.

AI investment spending should keep rising. But the AI spending boom might be 
less beneficial for GDP than it appears, however, as Europe’s AI-related trade 
deficit is widening as both AI -related exports weaken and AI -related imports 
grow.  
Monetary policy is tightening too, which will restrain growth with a lag  (see our 
latest review of upstream and downstream financial conditions here). Concerns 
about sovereign debt sustainability could generate additional growth -sapping 
risk premia.  
Finally, competitiveness remains weak and the Second China Shock  and 
increasingly direct competition with China could weaken European production 
and employment.  
The bottom line is the economy has been remarkably resilient to the energy shock 
so far and our euro area GDP forecasts are probably too low at 0.5% in 2026 and 
1.1% in 2027  (our German team have raised their 2026 forecast  from 0.5% to 
closer to 1.0%).  
Q2: Will the ECB stop at 2.50% or continue hiking until policy is 
restrictive? 
Since early March we have been expecting the ECB to hike twice (Jun and Sep), 
raising the policy rate to 2.50%, the upper end of the range of neutral. This felt 
like good risk management. It signals the ECB commitment to price stability on 
the one hand wit hout overly burdening economic growth in the context of a 
negative supply shock on the other. 
If growth was weakening  amid a supply shock , inflation expectations  were 
comprehensively anchored and data showed no risk of energy price pass -

### Page 4

3 September 2026 
Europe Blog

Deutsche Bank AG Page 4

through, it would be easier to call into question the ECB’s decision to hike rather 
than “look through” this energy supply shock.  
In the round, the data, the ECB messaging ( even this week Isabel Schnabel said 
the market “very well” understands the ECB reaction function , repeating what 
President Lagarde said in the July press conference) and the lack of resolution in 
the Middle East and still elevated energy prices mean a hike to 2.50% in 
September seems all but certain. Indeed, this is the impression from the July ECB 
Accounts. The question is, will the ECB stop there? 
There are two scenarios in which the ECB hikes to 2.75%.  
In one scenario, the ECB concludes that the upper end of the range of neutral is 
2.75% rather than 2.50%. The persistent strength of credit growth  in recent 
months despite higher lending rates and despite banks saying that lending 
standards are tightening might reveal that neutral is higher than expected. The 
scale of fiscal easing, even if led by Germany, could push r* higher. If this is right, 
the hurdle to raising policy rates to 2.75% might be lower than assumed and might 
not need strong or clear evidence of indirect or second-round inflation.

The argument is not one -sided. The strength of credit growth might reflect the 
lagged transmission of the earlier cuts rather than a higher neutral rate. In any 
case, our composite indicator of restrictiveness , based on a range of data that 
might reveal neutral, says that policy was still mildly restrictive in mid-2026 when 
the ECB started to hike. If that’s right, it might be more difficult to argue 2.75% is 
still within the range of neutral.

The other scenario for getting to 2.75% (or higher) is the ECB decides that a 
restrictive policy stance is appropriate. In the ECB’s current framework, this 
means moving from a “measured” tightening to a “forceful ” tightening. This 
requires the ECB to believe that the inflation shock is moving from a ‘large but not 
too persistent’ shock to a ‘more significant and sustained overshoot of the target’.  
There are different routes to believing in a more significant and sustained 
overshoot of the inflation target.  
One would be if energy prices remain at elevated levels for longer than expected. 
Oil prices are in line with baseline, but gas prices are more consistent with the 
adverse scenario (at least in the near term). Given the lagged transmission of 
rising gas pr ices into inflation, this could add persistence to the headline HICP 
shock, lengthening the period during which energy inflation could spillover into 
core.

Figure 7: Headline and core HICP: DB forecasts

Another way would be if larger than expected indirect effects and in particular 
second-round effects materialised. So far, there is no compelling evidence  of 
indirect effects and no evidence of second -round effects. However, both would 
only materialise with a lag. For example, it was six months before high energy-
sensitive core HICP started rising in the 2022 shock. 
A third way would be if stronger growth added persistence risk to inflation. The 
recent resilience of growth would add to this risk if it lasts. That said, if AI lifts 
productivity, the additional growth might not be as inflationary. 
How the ECB staff forecasts change in September will be very important to the 
outlook for monetary policy. In June, the baseline forecast had core HICP

### Page 6

3 September 2026 
Europe Blog

Deutsche Bank AG Page 6

averaging 2.5% in 2027 and staying at least 30bp above target through the year. 
Importantly, however, headline HICP was expected to be back down to the 2% 
target from Q3-27 and stay there through to the end of 2028. 
It is important to remember the baseline staff forecast in June was conditioned 
on policy rates rising to 2.75%. But despite sticky core inflation, it is difficult to 
imagine monetary policy tightening much further if headline inflation is 
sustainably returning to target within a year.  
As such, the near-term path for policy could depend critically on whether the 
Governing Council believes core inflation will converge down to headline in the 
medium term or headline converges up to core. The former is less hawkish, the 
latter is more hawkish.  
Which of these two outcomes seems more or less likely to the Governing Council 
will depend on how it judges the risk of inflation persistence. This is a function of 
pricing power, bargaining power and purchasing power. Our view is there is some 
risk of persistence, but not as much as 2022 and not enough to warrant an 
outright restrictive policy stance. Not yet, at least. 
Our 2.50% baseline might be too optimistic, but 3.00% feels too restrictive. 2.75% 
is a grey zone: a non-negligible possibility unless the situation in the Middle East 
is resolved quickly and energy prices normalise rapidly. Our work on policy rules 
shows that the ECB ‘baseline’ scenario macro forecast s are consistent  with 
2.75%. The ‘milder’ scenario forecasts are consistent with 2.50%. 
There may not be any new policy signals from the ECB at the Jackson Hole  
symposium. Unlike in the past, there is no global policymaker panel. Isabel 
Schnabel is the ECB member on the official agenda  – speaking about tokenized 
finance. However, markets will be on the watch for press interviews by ECB 
attendees. 
Q3: Will public finances and elections prove a destabilising 
combination? 
The environment around European public finances is becoming more challenging 
for a number of reasons. Government bond markets are under pressure globally, 
driven currently by the US. The ‘i minus g’ gap at the heart of debt dynamics is 
becoming less favourable  as the benefits of the low rates regime of the last 
decade expire, amplified by rising inflation risk, tighter monetary policy, rising 
term premia and generally low rates of economic growth.  
At the same time, public debt to GDP ratios have not returned to pre -pandemic 
levels, fiscal vulnerability is elevated even when accounting for improved external 
balances, and European governments are facing numerous spending demands, 
from defence and energy to climate change and technology.  
The combination of more vulnerable public finances and elections in several 
significant member states over the next 12 months creates the potential for 
tighter financial conditions. National elections lie ahead in Italy, Spain, Greece, 
Finland, Estonia, Poland and, perhaps most consequentially, in France. Financial 
conditions are well-behaved so far (the ECB’s state-of-the-art Macro-Finance FCI 
has hardly tightened) . The degree of tightening of financial conditions going

forward will be a function of the fiscal policy choices made by governments, the 
resilience (or not) of growth and the restrictiveness (or not) of ECB policy.

With regard to France, the opinion polls say that Rassemblement National (RN) 
has a credible path to winning the Presidency with Marine Le Pen. If that happens, 
there is a good chance an early legislative election will be called too.  
OAT markets have been on edge since President Macron’s ill -judged legislative 
election in 2024 resulted in a deeply divided parliament with little capacity to drive 
strong fiscal consolidation. On the basis of what is known at the moment, there is 
a lack of a credible consolidation trajectory in the RN fiscal plans. Even under 
moderately optimistic assumptions about consolidation, France’s debt ratio will 
likely remain broadly flat in the range of 120-125% of GDP over the next decade. 
A fl at trajectory means vulnerability to any new negative surprises on growth, 
funding costs and/or deficits – and as we have said before, the deficit is under 
pressure from inflation (indexed spending), legacy policy decisions (stalling the 
pension reform), defence, ageing, etc.  
Much will depend on the course of French fiscal policy , particularly RN’s fiscal 
policy. This could be revealed in three distinct stages over the year ahead. 
• The 2027 budget . The incumbent Lecornu government needs to have a 
budget agreed by year -end. A deeply divided parliament and 
approaching election will make strong consolidation difficult. 
Importantly, however, RN is signaling that it won’t oppose a budget. The 
alternative (“special law”) would mean a less stable financial position in 
2027. RN would prefer to have some breathing space if it is elected. 
• The election campaign. This will reveal more about RN’s fiscal plans. The 
limited details available so far imply a wider deficit. However, RN’s fiscal

identity is unclear and RN President Bardella’s admiration for Italy’s 
market-friendly fiscal stance under PM Meloni suggests an 
understanding of the benefits of a stable bond market. The extent of the

influence market confidence in the availability of European backstops 
(ECB TPI, ESM, etc).  
• Post-election decisions . Assuming an RN President would dissolve  
parliament in the spring to trigger legislative elections by the summer, 
the first task for the new parliament would be the preparation of the 2028 
budget. This assumes a budget for 2027 has already been passed. If 
France is operating under a special law, a 2027 budget would take 
immediate precedence. These would reveal RN’s fiscal plans and their 
consistency with EU rules.

Counterbalances. France will be the focal point for the market. As we have argued 
(here, here and here), the key theme for investors is not outright regime change, 
but a constant, volatility-inducing push and pull between RN’s political objectives 
– shifting in places – and the fiscal, legal, and market constraints that would 
ultimately shape its capacity to govern. The push for political change will generate 
friction.  
There are several buffers that could help keep the country off the more 
challenging paths.  
• The market is already pricing weak fundamentals . French yields have 
been gradually adjusting to these weak fundamentals over the last couple

### Page 9

3 September 2026 
Europe Blog

Deutsche Bank AG Page 9

of years. For example, the current yield already anticipated further credit 
downgrades.  
• Robust banking. French banks are well -capitalised and unlike the crisis -
hit periphery a decade and a half ago hold relatively little direct exposure 
to OATs. As a global safe asset, OATs are widely held abroad.  
• Backstops. Unlike the sovereign debt crisis 15 years ago, there are 
backstops in place. For example, the ESM can lend to crisis -hit member 
states and the ECB can intervene in the market for eligible member states 
with TPI. If foreign investors offload OATs in scale , the French banks 
have some capacity. However, as bank exposure increases, the potential 
for a more destabilising sovereign/banking doom-loop would increase.  
• Economic growth. There are several reasons why French economic 
growth could outperform its peers and help stabilise debt. One is AI. 
Among the larger member states, France is spending the most on digital 
investment and was the only EU member state to launch a notable AI 
model in 2025 (50 in the US). France is also relatively more exposed to 
defence production, and among the larger EU member states has 
relatively favourable demographics.

September 2, 2026 
The connectivity chokepoint 
— Digital connectivity—the transmission of data through terrestrial fibre, mobile 
networks, subsea cables and satellites—has become a critical arena of geopolitical 
competition. 
— The Internet’s physical infrastructure is increasingly viewed as a source of strategic 
leverage, resilience and vulnerability. 
— In the era of “infrastructure realism, ” the contest increasingly involves both states and 
hyperscalers such as Google, Meta, Microsoft and Amazon, whose AI and cloud 
ambitions are reshaping global connectivity infrastructure.

Digital connectivity has become a central arena of great-power competition. While states 
have long used communications infrastructure to project influence and control, in the age 
of AI, the contest to dominate digital networks has become a defining geopolitical frontier 
as countries and – increasingly – private actors look to win the AI race.1 
This chapter in our Infrastructure Realism series examines the physical systems that 
enable digital connectivity: undersea cables and communications satellites. We assess 
their role on the Internet—the largest and most consequential connectivity network—and 
the strategic advantages and vulnerabilities they create for state actors. 
Subsea cables are indispensable to the global economy. They carry more than 99% of 
intercontinental data traffic, making sabotage or prolonged disruption a direct threat to 
global Internet connectivity.2 Satellites carry only a small share of international data, but 
they are crucial for last-mile access where fiber cannot reach, particularly for 
governments and defense users. As we go on to explore, the rise of communications-
based satellites is also becoming integral to the global AI race. 
Several incidents have underscored the criticality of connectivity infrastructure. In April, 
reports of Russian surveillance near UK cables and pipelines raised concerns.3 In May, 
amid conflict in the Middle East, Iran threatened to sever undersea cables in the Red Sea. 
Iran’s IRGC, also floated the idea, through state-affiliated media, of charging foreign 
operators infrastructure fees and licensing royalties for routes through Iranian territorial 
waters.4 This would have significant implications for hyperscalers such as Meta, Google 
and Microsoft, which rely on these corridors for portions of their international traffic and 
cloud connectivity.

1 The first instance of communications sabotage was during WW1, when the British cut Germany’s undersea telegraph cables in 
the English Channel, forcing German telegraph traffic onto British-controlled networks that could be intercepted. And in the 21st 
century, autocratic states like Iran and Russia have imposed internet shutdowns to control their population / information 
access.   
2 The 99% does not account for total global internet traffic, given the vast majority of internet traffic is domestic and does not 
cross an ocean at all. Instead, the 99% applies to internet traffic that goes between continents. Mauldin, Alan. 2023. ‘Do 
Submarine Cables Account for Over 99% of Intercontinental Data Traffic?’ . In Telegeography.Com. 
3 Olivia Ireland, Thomas Copeland, and Frank Gardner, “UK Says Russia Ran Submarine Operation over Cables and Pipelines, ” 
BBC News, April 9, 2026, https://www.bbc.co.uk/news/articles/cre13qn9z7do. 
4 “IRGC Media Float Plan to Levy Fees for Subsea Internet Cables, ” Amwaj. Media, 2020, https://amwaj.media/en/media-

Section 1 explains how the Internet works and traces the physical journey of data, 
highlighting the points at which state disruption can occur. We give less emphasis to 5G, 
due to our definition of what constitutes the Internet’s infrastructure. This is because while  
states—most notably China—have used 5G equipment to project influence, 5G is 
primarily an access network: it links mobile devices to the wider Internet but does not 
replace the role of subsea cables and satellites.  
Sections 2 and 3 analyse subsea cables and communications satellites, detailing the 
strategic advantages and vulnerabilities of each ecosystem and how states are seeking to 
consolidate their influence. Finally, Section 4 examines the rise of private actors such as 
Google and Meta, which are reshaping global connectivity by building their own data 
corridors and controlling more of the Internet stack to meet rising demand.

### Page 3

Deutsche Bank Research Institute 
The Great Rebalancing (4/5): The connectivity chokepoint – September 2, 2026

3

1. How connectivity works  
1.1 What is the Internet? 
The Internet enables global communication, data exchange and access to information 
through a system of cables, satellites and wireless technologies. It is not a single entity but 
a common set of protocols that allows independently owned networks to communicate. In 
this sense, it is a “network of networks” through which data moves across billions of 
devices. 
To move across the Internet, data is broken into small “packets” and routed across 
different systems using standard protocols. The primary ones are the Transmission 
Control Protocol (TCP), which ensures the reliability of the connection and data, and the 
Internet Protocol (IP), which acts as an addressing system to find and route packets to 
their destination.5 Using these protocols, data travels through a global web of fiber-optic 
cables that connect major network hubs, forming what is known as the Internet Backbone. 
The process is as follows:  
— A packet leaves the user’s device and enters a local access network.6 Over fixed 
broadband, the data packet passes through a Wi-Fi router and then to an Internet 
service provider (ISP). Over mobile broadband, 5G carries it to a radio base station 
and the mobile operator’s core network before it reaches the wider Internet. 
— From the ISP , the packet is passed from one junction (or router) to the next, 
crossing multiple networks toward its destination server.   
— If the destination server is nearby, the packet may be exchanged at a local Internet 
Exchange Point (IXP). If it is distant, the ISP routes it onto the backbone, across a 
country or ocean, and into an ISP at the other end. 
— The response takes a similar path back, with each router along the way making 
forwarding decisions based on the destination IP address.  
How one network gets its traffic to the rest of the Internet, and whether it must pay for it, is 
where key corporations come in. There are two ways networks connect: 1) through transit, 
where a smaller network pays a larger one to carry its data across the globe, and 2) 
through peering, where two networks of similar size agree to exchange traffic directly at no 
cost. Within this system, a hierarchy of companies has emerged.  
At the top are Tier 1 network operators, which can reach the entire Internet through 
settlement-free peering. This small group includes major telecom operators such as 
Lumen, AT&T, Tata Communications, NTT, Arelion, GTT and Cogent. 
Below these are Tier 2 network operators, such as regional carriers and internet service 
providers like Comcast and Vodafone. These entities primarily purchase transit from Tier 1 
providers to reach parts of the internet they cannot access via peering. Finally, Tier 3 
network operators rely exclusively on paying for transit from Tier 2s to connect their 
customers. These are typically the local ISPs that provide Internet service to homes or 
offices. Therefore, a data packet from a user’s laptop typically travels from a Tier 3 provider 
to a Tier 2, onto a Tier 1 backbone, across an undersea cable to another Tier 1 network, 
and then down through the tiers to its destination server.   
1.2 How data physically travels  
The Internet ultimately depends on physical infrastructure. As we said in step 1 above, the 
first leg is not always a cable, with mobile technologies like 5G carrying data wirelessly 
between the user’s device and a nearby base station. Satellite systems can also provide a

5 Rus Shuler, “How Does the Internet Work?, ” Stanford.Edu, 2002, https://web.stanford.edu/class/msande91si/www-
spr04/readings/week1/InternetWhitepaper.htm. 
6 Access networks connect the user’s device or premises to the first point in an Internet or telecom provider’s network. They can 
be wired or wireless, and in addition to 5G and Wi-FI mentioned above, other examples are fiber-to-home, cable broadband, and 
satellite broadband.

### Page 4

Deutsche Bank Research Institute 
The Great Rebalancing (4/5): The connectivity chokepoint – September 2, 2026

4

wireless connection over much longer distances. Beyond that access layer, however, both 
systems typically rely on terrestrial fibre and the wider Internet backbone. Most long-
distance traffic travels as pulses of light through fibre-optic cables buried on land or laid 
across the seabed, and each glass strand is thinner than a human hair. 
Terrestrial cables handle domestic and regional internet traffic, while undersea fiber-optic 
cables carry intercontinental internet and telecom traffic. When a user sends data across 
the globe, it travels through local terrestrial cables to a coastal landing station. This station 
then transmits the data across the ocean via a subsea cable to a corresponding landing 
station on another continent, which receives the data and sends it through local terrestrial 
cables to the destination server.   
Satellites complement cables, providing connectivity in remote or disrupted environments 
where fiber-optic cables cannot reach. There are two different types: traditional satellites, 
which sit in a fixed, high-altitude orbit, offering broad coverage but with a noticeable delay 
due to the long signal path. A more recent approach, exemplified by services like Starlink, 
uses large constellations of thousands of satellites flying much lower to the earth to 
reduce latency and improve service.   
1.3 How the Internet transitioned from a public good to a private advantage 
Similar to  crypto’s arc  discussed in this series (here), the Internet came to symbolise 
decentralisation during the globalisation and commercialisation of the 1990s and 2000s. 
Its ability to reroute traffic reinforced the perception that it was neutral and 
disintermediated. Yet the network depends on physical assets concentrated among a 
limited number of carriers—and increasingly hyperscalers—that control key digital 
corridors and possess extensive visibility over network traffic. 
Conceived as a “network of networks, ” the Internet began as a US government initiative in 
the 1960s to facilitate information sharing among research universities.7 Its predecessor, 
ARPANET (Advanced Research Projects Agency Network), became the first operational 
“packet-switching network” in 1969. Famously, its first attempted message – “LOGIN” – 
crashed the system after only the letters “LO” appeared, an event now widely regarded as 
the moment the Internet was born. Over the following decades, public researchers 
cultivated the Internet ecosystem by developing concepts like email, data packet 
switching, the World Wide Web service (WWW), and the protocols needed to create the 
Internet ecosystem, all with the goal of keeping the Internet open for all.   
By the 1990s, the emergence of major internet exchange providers and the release of 
WWW software into the public domain enabled millions of people to use the Internet 
freely.8 As private interest in commercializing the Internet grew, the US Congress passed 
legislation in 1993 that opened access to Internet applications like computing and 
networking to private enterprise, which had previously only been available to universities 
and government agencies. This led to thousands of firms entering the market to pursue 
commercial ventures using TCP/IP technologies.   
By 1995, the government agency responsible for overseeing the Internet, NSFNET, was 
decommissioned and replaced by four privately operated Network Access Points in 
Washington, New York, Chicago, and California. This marked the Internet’s transition from 
a government-funded academic experiment to a fully private enterprise. The dot-com 
bubble in the late 1990s and eventual collapse of Internet stocks in the 2000s cleared the 
way for the companies that today define the modern Internet, including Amazon (1994), 
eBay (1995), Google (1998) and Facebook (2004). The more recent roll-out of broadband,

7 The Department of Defense created ARPA (the Advanced Research Projects Agency) in 1958 in an attempt to lead technological 
breakthroughs for the military. It was replaced by NSFNET as the main US research network for the Internet in the 1980s. 
8 The Internet is the global infrastructure of interconnected computers and networks, while the WWW is a service running on that 
infrastructure, using web browsers to access linked hypertext documents.

### Page 5

Deutsche Bank Research Institute 
The Great Rebalancing (4/5): The connectivity chokepoint – September 2, 2026

5

fiber-to-the-home, and 3G/4G/5G cellular networks has also helped make the Internet 
ubiquitous and increasingly mobile.  
1.4 Why a decentralized system still creates concentration  
No single entity owns the Internet, but a small number of companies and countries control 
the infrastructure on which it depends on. As a result, the Internet’s ecosystem remains 
highly concentrated across few backbone carriers and transit providers, telecom-
equipment suppliers, cable landing stations, and internet exchange points to name a few, 
thereby creating numerous chokepoints that different countries have raced to ensure a 
foothold in.9  
Beyond telecom equipment, the physical layer of digital connectivity is also concentrated. 
Subsea-cable ownership spans telecom operators, consortia, state-backed entities and 
technology companies, yet control over capacity, construction, strategic routes and 
landing points is narrowing. Cable ownership is distinct from Tier 1 backbone status, but 
the boundary is blurred because operators such as AT&T and Tata also own subsea 
infrastructure or capacity. 
Today, this landscape is also shifting as large content providers have become the 
dominant users of international bandwidth and increasingly fund, co-own or purchase 
large allocations of new subsea cable capacity. As we will explain more in section 4, this is 
flattening the traditional hierarchy, since under these private backbones, hyperscalers are 
reducing their reliance on traditional Tier 1 transit hierarchy by peering directly with 
consumer ISPs and even placing their own servers inside those ISP networks. 
Figure 1: Notable chokepoints in the connectivity corridor 
   Selected key players  
Subsea cables (suppliers)   SubCom, Nippon Electric Company (NEC), HMN Technologies, Alcatel 
Submarine Networks (ASN)   
Subsea cables (owners)  Hyperscalers (Google, Meta, Microsoft, Amazon) and Telecom carriers 
(Orange, Tata, Vodafone, Telstra, China Telecom, China Mobile)

2. Undersea cables: The invisible backbone of the internet 
2.1 How undersea cables work  
Subsea cables are long, fiber-optic conduits laid on the seabed that, in total, stretch for 
more than 1.8 million kilometers. There are approximately 500 active submarine cables 
around the world, and while many are barely thicker than a garden hose, yet they carry 
over 99% of all intercontinental data traffic. Their dominance is due to their high capacity, 
low latency and reliability, which make them ideal for transmitting vast volumes of data 
and information quickly. While most of the world’s cables are laid by just four companies – 
SubCom (US), Alcatel Submarine Networks (France), Nippon Electric Company (Japan),

9 The geopolitical case of China’s Huawei is one example. As the company that provided many of the physical and software 
components needed for 5G, Huawei rapidly ascended to become a significant part of the Internet’s underlying infrastructure, and 
thus a critical player in global digital connectivity. This later proved to be a problem during accelerating tensions between China 
and Western countries, as many nations sought to disentangle themselves from Huawei’s supply chains in their telecom 
networks.

### Page 6

Deutsche Bank Research Institute 
The Great Rebalancing (4/5): The connectivity chokepoint – September 2, 2026

6

and HMN Technologies (formerly Huawei Marine Networks; China) – it is US hyperscalers 
who now finance and control most of the new cable capacity.10  
Today, subsea cables are the foundation of the global telecommunications industry 
because they transfer data and voice traffic more cheaply and quickly than any alternative. 
Yet for much of the 20th century, the bulk of transoceanic traffic was primarily carried by 
satellites. The transition only began in 1988 with the installation of TAT-8, the first trans-
oceanic fibre-optic cable, which linked the US, the UK and France. This marked the point 
at which submarine cables began to outperform satellites in volume, speed and cost-
effectiveness of data and voice communications.11 Consequently, almost all transoceanic 
telecommunications are now routed via the submarine cable network, with satellites 
handling only around 3% of total traffic now, according to ESCAEU.12

Figure 2: Global submarine cables, with the most geopolitically contentious corridors

Source: TeleGeography, www.submarinecablemap.com, Deutsche Bank Research. 
2.2 Why undersea cables are strategic  
Beyond serving as the Internet’s backbone, these underwater cables are essential for 
global trade, finance, cloud computing, and government communications. For example, 
submarine cables can carry around 375mn simultaneous telephone conversations on 1 
fiber optic strand.13 The SWIFT network also relies on cables for transmitting financial data 
to more than 8,300 member financial institutions in 195 countries.14 It is also estimated 
that $10trn worth of financial transactions travel through these subsea cables every day.15  
A 2026 study found that undersea cable disruption also had massive macroeconomic 
consequences for global economies.16 Specifically, a typical cable disruption often 
triggers an immediate 2ppt decline in GDP capita growth for an economy. There is also 
evidence to show that the impact continues to accumulate over time; within three to six

telecoms-industry-report/.

years, the affected country’s economy diverges, falling 9 ppts below its non-disrupted 
neighbors. These disruptions also reduce overall economic efficiency, innovation and 
labor productivity, followed by a tightening of financial conditions as domestic credit for 
non-financial private agents’ contracts and cross-border banking positions weaken. The 
study also noted that in countries with fewer international cable relations and/or without 
internet exchange points, disruptions widen the GDP per capita gap immediately and 
substantially over the medium run, compared to nearby non-disrupted economies that 
instead experience offsetting gains consistent with reallocations.17  
These findings highlight a broader point: the resilience of subsea cable infrastructure is 
not merely a telecommunications issue, but a broader precondition for economic stability 
and the functioning of modern financial systems.  
The strategic importance of these networks is further amplified by the geography of the 
routes themselves. Because international data traffic is concentrated within a limited 
number of physical corridors, damage to key cable systems could have magnanimous 
consequences. One example that illustrates this point is to consider a  high-frequency 
trader in London pulling live market data from a server on Wall Street in New York. The 
request would be transmitted as packets across terrestrial fibre networks in the UK, where 
it enters a low-latency transatlantic cable system. Examples of such routes include EXA 
Infrastructure’s Express system, which forms part of the low-latency route between New 
York and London or Aqua Comm’s AEC-1 linking New York and Ireland, and then on to the 
UK.  
Many of these transatlantic cables pass through the GIUK Gap in the Atlantic, a 
geopolitically sensitive maritime corridor between Greenland, Iceland, and the UK. The 
passage is contentious because it is a critical chokepoint for NATO maritime surveillance 
and because it is also increasingly a primary route for Russia’s Northern Fleet to access 
the Atlantic. So this concentration of critical data infrastructure within a militarily 
significant area creates a potential vulnerability.  
As such, subsea cable infrastructure security has become a national priority for several 
countries, including the EU, US, Japan, India and Australia.   
2.3 Undersea cable vulnerabilities  
The criticality of subsea cables is heightened by the fact that they can be vulnerable to 
damage, outages and cyber risks, such as natural or man-made hazards, and hazards to 
other infrastructure including the landing stations and IT systems that support the cables. 
This systemic risk is amplified because many global networks rely on a limited number of 
routers, hubs and suppliers.   
Crucially, there is often no “Plan B” when a major cable is disrupted, and there are a 
reported 150-200 cable faults recorded annually.18 The challenge is threefold: first, repairs 
are immensely costly, estimated at $500,000-$1mn per incident for a subsea fibre optic 
cable, and $10 -$100mn per incident for a subsea power cable.19 Second, there are only 
~40 specialist cable repair vessels in the entire world, owned by a handful of companies 
like SubCom, Orange Marine, and HMN Technologies. These vessels operate on a first 
come first served basis within the consortium’s zone agreement. And while the repair 
ships can mobilize within a 24-hour period, repair time frames vary according to fault 
location, while the requirement to secure repair permits within territorial or exclusive 
economic zone waters can be burdensome and significantly delay starting operations.20 
Finally, these cables are often concentrated in physical chokepoints – such as along

shallow straits or continental shelves – that are also areas of geopolitical strife, making

While direct evidence of deliberate cable sabotage has been hard to prove thus far, several 
incidents of damage to critical undersea infrastructure sites have caught the attention of 
governments.21 The US Congress, in its proposed Strategic Subsea Cables Act of 2026, 
noted damage to cables connecting the Norwegian mainland with the Norwegian 
archipelago of Svalbard (2022), multiple cuts to cables in the Taiwan Strait (2023, 2025), 
and disruptions to cables in the Baltic Sea (2024, 2026) as key examples of this growing 
threat.22  
Perhaps the more immediate concern for individual users was the threat that emerged 
earlier this year, when Iran threatened to cut its undersea cables in the Red Sea. Since 
these routes carry around 17-25% of global internet traffic, the ramifications of such an act 
would be severe.23 Even before this threat, subsea cable operators in the Red Sea have 
been plagued with numerous problems. Yemeni territorial disputes and Houthi attacks on 
shipping lanes have obstructed the deployment of several new cables – including the 
2Africa cable, which is almost complete, and other cables such as the IEX, Africa-1, and 
Raman, which are still awaiting deployment. The geopolitical strife in the corridor has led 
to recent reports that all cable projects there have been “delayed indefinitely. ”24  
Geopolitical risk is rising, but most cable damage remains accidental. The International 
Cable Protection Committee estimates that fishing gear and ship anchors account for 
roughly 70–80% of faults. 
2.4 State actors responding 
Governments have long regarded submarine telecommunications networks as critical 
infrastructure requiring strong protection (for example, ACMA, 2007).25 More recently, they 
have also recognized the geopolitical value of controlling these corridors. In 2015, China 
set a goal of capturing 60% of the fibre-optic cable market through state-backed firms 
under its Digital Silk Road initiative.26 HMN Technologies (formerly Huawei Marine) is one 
of four companies that collectively dominate global cable manufacturing. 
However, China’s influence is primarily concentrated in the Global South, as Chinese 
firms have been largely excluded from most Western-connected systems since the US 
launched its 2020 Clean Network program to counter China’s influence across telecoms 
and data. Under the Clean Network, Chinese telecom and cable firms were shut out of 
lucrative bids for cable-laying projects, most notably when HMN Technologies lost the 
SEA-ME-WE 6 project (running from France to Singapore via the Suez) in 2023 despite its 
competitive pricing. aChina has pivoted to financing new cable projects connecting Africa, 
Asia and the Middle East, such as its flagship PEACE cable (Pakistan-East Africa-Europe) 
and EMA (Europe-Middle East-Asia) project, which is backed by three state-owned telcos: 
China Telecom, China Mobile, and China Unicom in partnership with Egypt and Saudi 
Arabia.   
Over the last few years, the US has consolidated its toolkit to protect its subsea cable 
security.  In February of this year, the US Congress introduced the bipartisan Strategic
